2026-07-27⭐ SHOP**/wiki-ingest (Outlook). 1 intra-quarter row. RATING CHANGE, logged per the thesis-drift rule: Rothschild & Co / Redburn DOWNGRADES SHOP TO NEUTRAL (from Buy).** **What makes this worth more than a rating line is that the downgrade is entirely derivative of a [[META]] thesis — Redburn's new Internet analyst (Dominic Ball) launched 100+ pages arguing Meta's end-to-end SMB platform turns it 'from AI Spender to AI winner', and 'success here will likely come AT THE EXPENSE OF SHOPIFY.'** **The threat is specified rather than hand-waved, which is why it belongs on this page in detail: Meta's SMB LLM (codenamed 'Watermelon') would let a merchant PHOTOGRAPH A PRODUCT, SELECT A TARGET MARKET, and have Meta build the STOREFRONT, CATALOGUE, PRICING, CREATIVE AND CRM — then feed the data and advertising straight into Advantage+.** Redburn sizes it at **~$80bn of SMB-LLM revenue by 2030 on 30% penetration of the 250 MILLION BUSINESSES ALREADY ON META'S PLATFORM**, with the bundled stack delivering **~25% higher return on ad spend than standalone alternatives** — i.e. the displacement argument rests on an ROAS advantage Shopify structurally cannot match, not on price. **The conclusion, verbatim: 'If Meta internalizes the storefront and commerce stack FROM H2, Shopify's growth could slow MATERIALLY, and at 50x P/E, the risk/reward no longer looks attractive.'** **Two things to hold against it. First, the timing claim ('from H2') and the $80bn/2030 sizing are Redburn's model, not disclosed Meta plans — Watermelon has not been announced as a product. Second, and cutting the other way, independent corroboration arrived the SAME DAY: Meta's Chief AI Officer Alexandr Wang told YC Startup School that Meta will build a HARNESS for its models plus open-source LLMs — precisely the agentic framework an SMB storefront product requires — and that 'the best AI products are yet to come'.** So the capability build is real and dated even if the commerce product is not yet announced. **The number that decides it is the 50x P/E: at that multiple the stock is priced for uninterrupted SMB share, and the debate is no longer about Shopify's execution but about whether its largest traffic source becomes its competitor.** Also noted: SHOP appeared on CNBC's biggest-analyst-calls list 07-22. Cross-filed [[META]], [[GOOG]].

and UBS's **head-to-head scorecard — EMIB-T wins architecture simplicity, package complexity, scalability >12x reticle, cost (assuming reasonable yield) and time-to-market; CoPoS wins performance, power integrity, yield confidence and ecosystem; manufacturing risk a TIE** — plus a rarely-cited Intel edge, its **SMT expertise for board-level warpage management**. (6) **[[SMIC]] CEO Zhao Haijun said on the May earnings call that overseas customers were looking to make chips in China because capacity was tight elsewhere** — the offsetting demand pull, and expansion that itself needs litho; (7) the domestic tool initially serves **mature nodes including power-management components essential to AI servers/data centers**; (8) the structural history — **lithography a Chinese national priority since 2002; Nothing superseded** (Bernstein Outperform €2,500 stands). **The material item is a same-day, market-moving China datapoint with a quantified stock reaction: ASML fell AS MUCH AS 6.6% and closed down ~5% Monday after The Information reported that Shanghai Yuliansheng Technology, a Chinese state-backed company, has STARTED MANUFACTURING IMMERSION LITHOGRAPHY MACHINES for delivery to [[SMIC]], Hua Hong and CXMT — five systems this year and around 20 in 2027.** **What makes it more than a headline: reports last year had SMIC merely TRIALLING a Yuliansheng immersion tool, so if accurate this marks the move from trial to production delivery.** **Exposure sizing, which the page needed: immersion litho sales to China are estimated at a LOW-TEENS PERCENTAGE of ASML's total system sales.** **Deliberately logged with both of Jefferies' hedges intact rather than as settled fact: (1) 'there have been FALSE REPORTS of Chinese progress in immersion lithography previously and it remains to be seen if this is a valid report'; (2) even if domestic substitution disappoints, the MATCH ACT moving through the House and Senate would BAN ASML immersion-litho sales to China if passed — so the same revenue line is exposed from two independent directions.** Beavington's balanced read is that China keeps buying ASML while it can on quality, but **domestic capability compounds faster once majors use these tools in COMMERCIAL production rather than trials**, and he expects the newsflow to **keep pressuring ASML's valuation multiple** — which is exactly what Monday's tape did. **The offsetting item, and the two should be held together: UBS ranks ASML at the TOP of the pricing hierarchy for this SPE earnings season (most forceful on price, alongside [[AMAT]]; [[LRCX]] measured; [[KLAC]] least likely), frames the season as being about CAPACITY EXPANSIONS AND MARGIN TRAJECTORY rather than quarterly numbers, and cites ASML's strong print plus commentary that customer conversations are already shifting into C2028.** Net: **the pricing and duration legs of the thesis strengthened in the same session the China volume leg was challenged** — and the low-teens China exposure is the number that bounds the damage. 2026-07-26⭐ TSM**4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (Fubon Buy NT$3,500, UBS Buy, Nomura Buy, BofA all stand). **The headline is that the EMIB-T threat finally got stress-tested by a house that covers both sides, and TSMC wins in the bull case for Intel.** UBS sizes the **advanced-packaging TAM at US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR**, and then hands Intel its best plausible outcome — **13% industry share / US$16bn of sales by 2030, packages for ~24mn accelerator dies, with CoPoS not reaching MP for Nvidia until 2028 and for others until 2029-30** — and TSMC *still* compounds advanced-packaging sales at a **50% CAGR 2026-30, holds 65-70% share, and takes advanced packaging to 18% of total sales in 2030E from 9% in 2026E.** In UBS's *lower* case (stronger EMIB-T adoption) that becomes **47% CAGR / 62% share / 16% of sales** — i.e. **the base-vs-bear spread is roughly 2pts of revenue mix over four years.** That is the most useful bound on packaging-disruption risk this page has, and it argues the debate is worth far less to TSMC's numbers than to Intel's. **Not smoothed over: UBS also says EMIB-T reaches MP in H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028, and names TSMC's own three execution risks — "CoPoS warpage, panel process uniformity, and new equipment readiness."** The compensating technical case: CoPoS offers "tighter interconnect, better signal integrity, broader integration options such as 3D stacking (and eventually, CPO)"; EMIB-T's edge is structural cost (no interposer) and scalability to ultra-large packages on large substrates. UBS's own framing of the outcome — **EMIB-T as "a meaningful second-source for selected hyperscaler ASICs" while CoPoS stays "the preferred platform for customers prioritizing the highest performance and execution reliability"** — is a share-of-mind concession, not a share-of-revenue one. **Second, and it corroborates the above from a completely different seat: an ex-ASM International senior process engineer (AlphaSense expert call, 07-25) independently concludes that TSMC's dominance holds and that the binding constraint is TOOL availability — specifically bonding tools — "not… financial or market factors,"** with demand for CoWoS and CoPoS "outpacing supply for the foreseeable future," the market **bifurcated (TSMC in complex, OSATs in mature)**, and the TSMC-NVIDIA relationship "deeply integrated… making it difficult for competitors to break in." Caveat kept on the page: it is a 2-page expert-network summary and the expert's background is front-end ALD/process integration, so it is a well-informed prior rather than a supply-chain audit. **Third — a pattern worth naming rather than a one-off: on glass-core substrates TSMC is ALSO behind Intel.** MS's checks have **Innolux + TSMC + Ibiden reaching MP only in 2H28 or 2029, versus Intel/Lens Tech with a pilot line by end-2026 and meaningful volume from 2H27** — the same ~12-18-month ordering as EMIB-T vs CoPoS. On both bridges and substrates, Intel is earlier and TSMC is betting on the higher-performance version arriving later. Glass core is directly relevant because it attacks **warpage**, one of the three CoPoS risks UBS names. **Fourth, restated because it is management's own words and was not on this page in this form: "TSMC highlighted at its 2Q26 call that back-end sits in deeper shortage than front-end, with packaging capacity so tight it now limits customer growth"** (via Jefferies) — i.e. packaging, not wafers, is the named growth constraint, which is the demand-side justification for the entire capex build above, plus **two ADDITIONAL advanced-packaging fabs announced on the call** (a positive read to [[KLAC]]'s Advanced Packaging SPC line, C26 forecast $1B / +57% y/y). Minor housekeeping note: BofA's weekend desk recap carried TSMC 2026 capex as "$58B" in a one-line summary while its own semicap section used **$60-64B from $52-56B** — the **$60-64B guide already confirmed on this page (07-16 call) stands**; the $58B is a modelled/actual figure, not the guide. Cross-filed [[INTC]], [[NVDA]], [[KLAC]], [[MEDIATEK]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/optical-cpo](themes/optical-cpo.md).

Against all of it, memory was the best-performing sub-sector on the tape last week (**MU +8%, WDC +9%, STX +8%, SNDK +6%**) while the **SOX sat -19% off its June high** — the pricing checks (**+15-20% m/m July PC DRAM contract, implying +30-40% q/q vs TrendForce's +13-18%**) are being paid for even as the sector derates. 2026-07-26⭐ SAMSUNG(/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch into the 7/29 print). 5 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** — but the page now carries what is, on its face, **the most consequential single datapoint of the quarter: Samsung announced a $200bn / 5-year chip supply contract with [[AVGO]] covering memory, foundry AND advanced packaging** (Reuters, 2026-07-25: "Samsung Elec wins $200 billion Broadcom AI chip partnership, boosting foundry push"). It matters for three reasons, in order: **(1) it is a foundry win**, and the standing bear on this page has been precisely that Samsung Foundry cannot land a marquee leading-edge logic customer at scale — Broadcom is the largest merchant custom-ASIC house there is; **(2) it bundles DRAM/HBM + foundry + advanced packaging into one counterparty**, which is the "turnkey" pitch Samsung has been making against the TSMC-plus-separate-OSAT model, now with a reference customer; **(3) ~$40bn/yr against Samsung's current foundry run-rate is transformational and, if it converts, forces the capex line materially higher** — BofA's desk drew that inference the same day ("bodes well for Samsung capex"), which makes it a WFE read-through as well ([[KLAC]] / [[LRCX]] / [[AMAT]] / [[ASML]], filed to [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md)). **Deliberately NOT treated as a booked number:** as reported it is a supply-contract/partnership *value*, not backlog; no node, product or phasing split has been disclosed; and BofA's own weekend note hedged it as "AVGO **possibly** doing $200B deal with Samsung foundry," i.e. the desk was reading the same press we are. **Management gets its first chance to size or qualify it on 7/29 — that is the thing to watch, not the headline.** One cross-read worth holding: Hock Tan told JPM on 07-07 that Broadcom keeps majority TPU volume with Google, so this plausibly attaches to non-Google ASIC programs and/or HBM/packaging supply rather than implying a Google-share shift. **Second thread — the AMD content position is now explicitly two-sided and both sides stay on the page:** Jefferies reports Samsung "currently supplies HBM4 for AMD's MI455 Instinct GPUs and serves as the dedicated supplier of DDR5 solutions for the 6th-generation EPYC CPUs deployed in the Helios platform," while SemiAnalysis (07-21) documented a quantified HBM-content cut on a Meta-specific cut-down MI part and (07-24) that MSFT "dropped AMD after MI300X due to unreliable Samsung 2023 HBM memory." Named flagship supplier and carrier of the quality stigma on the same account — the **dedicated DDR5 sole-source language on 6th-gen EPYC is the cleaner win**. **Third: an [[AAPL]] System-LSI content gain — Samsung (with Novatek) takes iPhone 18 display-driver-IC share from LX Semicon, whose iPhone DDI share goes from 50%± (2022-25) to ~10%± on iPhone 18 (so ~90%+ to Novatek/Samsung), on Pro / Pro Max / foldable**, and for a technical reason (iPhone 18 "requires more advanced power/heating control") rather than price — which makes it more durable (BofA/Simon Woo, 07-26; LXS downgraded to Underperform, PO W63,000 → W25,000). Also logged: **South Korea's ~$950bn of new AI initiatives** announced around President Lee's US tour (Reuters, 07-25), and BofA reiterating both its **memory-bull stance into "a big week for memory — CXMT IPO, Kioxia, Samsung"** and its expectation of a **massive buyback + earlier dividends at the 7/29 call** (already on page from 07-24). 2026-07-26⭐ QCOM**/wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch into the 7/29 AMC print). 2 intra-quarter rows. Nothing superseded — but the page now carries a direct factual counter to the mechanism behind yesterday's PT cut, and the two need reconciling at the print rather than averaging.** **Bloomberg reported on 07-24 that "Qualcomm tells customers that prices are going up by double digits" — QCOM "planning to push through significant price increases for its chips."** Set that against the 07-25 entry below: **UBS cut its PT $235 → $190 and C27 revenue/EPS to $44.8B/$11.31 from $48.6B/$13.03 explicitly because memory-cost inflation bifurcates the handset market and QCOM cannot pass it on** (Apple/Samsung absorb, Chinese Android does not). **If double-digit hikes are actually going to customers, the pass-through assumption in that cut is wrong or premature.** Two reasons not to treat the Bloomberg item as a clean positive: (1) **announced ≠ realised** — the whole UBS argument is that the low-end Chinese Android base cannot absorb it, so the risk transfers from margin to volume/share (and [[MEDIATEK]] is the obvious share beneficiary); (2) the report is framed as a macro inflation datapoint, not a QCOM guidance item. **The honest state: QCOM was marked down for cost inflation and reported to be pushing that cost through within the same week, and the 7/29 call is where the two get settled.** Worth noting QCOM would be ahead of the analog complex on this — Jefferies says price increases have "not yet become a driver of revenue and margin broadly in the sector," with **[[IFX]]** expected to capture it first. **Second row, a new competitive framing of the data-center story: Morgan Stanley, in its MediaTek preview, asks about "new Qualcomm competition in ASIC design service"** — the first time on this page that QCOM's DC ambition is described as **custom-silicon design services** (competing for hyperscaler mandates against Broadcom/MediaTek/Alchip) rather than merchant inference parts (the HBC XPU on Azure, already on file from the June Investor Day). Credible on the Nuvia/packaging assets and a far larger TAM (MS puts the **2027 TPU revenue TAM alone at US$100bn**), but it is the third articulation of the DC strategy in eighteen months and it arrives as a question posed to a *competitor*, not as a QCOM disclosure. Watch the 7/29 call. Tape context: **ARM -36% and QCOM's peer group under pressure since the Tech-Momentum ATH; the SOX -19% off its June high with CPUs derating -21%.** Cross-filed [[MEDIATEK]], [[AAPL]], [[IFX]], [[AVGO]].
2026-07-26OPENAI**4 intra-quarter rows. Private co — nothing superseded, and deliberately NOT added to the compute-obligations table.** **The item of the weekend: WSJ reports Nvidia is in talks to provide a $250bn backstop for OpenAI, to help it lease a 10GW Ohio data-center project from SoftBank, with the project costing ~$500bn including chips.** Structurally this is different from everything already in the obligations table (**Oracle ~$300bn, Azure ~$250bn, ~$650bn total**): in this one **OpenAI is not the party carrying the balance-sheet risk — SoftBank owns the asset, NVDA guarantees the lease, OpenAI takes the capacity, NVDA books the chip revenue.** It is the GPU-debt-backstop mechanism from SemiAnalysis's "AI Project Trinity" (07-06, filed on [[NVDA]]) scaled to a single site and a single tenant, at roughly an order of magnitude beyond anything previously disclosed. **Two consequences: (1) if it converts, it materially reduces the equity and credit OpenAI itself must raise to secure 10GW — the tightest constraint in the buildout as modelled on this page; (2) it sharpens the circular-financing critique, because NVDA would be credit-enhancing its own customer's landlord.** JPM's read is a market test, not a catalyst — "if another $250bn guarantee isn't enough, then well… you really have to wonder what is" — with the SOX already -19% off its June high. **Status: talks per WSJ; unconfirmed by OpenAI, NVDA or SoftBank; the $250bn is NOT carried as a commitment and the obligations table is unchanged pending disclosure.** **Second: Altman travels to Washington this week to preview OpenAI's most powerful models** (Axios) — in the same week the administration is deciding how to treat Chinese open-weight models, which is unlikely to be coincidental. **Third, and logged with the tension intact: NYT reports OpenAI and [[ANTHROPIC]] are lobbying the White House to restrict Chinese open-weight models "despite what they may say," being "increasingly 'rattled' by China's advances," with the administration "leaning towards regulating Chinese models individually as a national security issue instead of issuing a blanket ban" — while NVDA published an open letter (MSFT co-leading) urging Washington NOT to impose sweeping restrictions.** The split is now cleanly along the stack: compute wants open weights free, the model layer wants them constrained. Per-model national-security review looks like the landing zone. **Fourth, pricing and — more usefully — same-benchmark cost: gpt-5.6-sol at ~$5.00/$30.00 per M in/out (≤272K input), between Fable 5 ($10/$50) and Grok 4.5 ($2/$6); but on total cost to run the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index the frontier tier (Fable 5 $5,631 / Opus 4.8 $3,753) sits far above open weights (Kimi-K3 $2,710, qwen3.7-plus $311, deepseek-v4-flash $74).** JPM's structural finding is the important one for pricing power: on Epoch AI's ECI the top-3 open vs top-3 closed gap has been **"relatively stable… rather than materially converging"** — the open models are cheaper, not catching up. OpenAI sits inside the routing layers designed to send simple work elsewhere (**MSFT Foundry Model Router, Intuit GenOS**), and WSJ (07-26) reports CTOs "reserving only the most specialized and complex queries for OpenAI and Anthropic." Dedup: the ~$750bn through-2030 compute-spend figure and the Hugging Face containment item were both filed 07-24/07-25 and were not re-added. Cross-filed [[NVDA]], [[ANTHROPIC]], [[MSFT]], [[ORCL]], [themes/tokenmaxxing](themes/tokenmaxxing.md).

2026-07-26⭐ NVDA(/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 6 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (MS Top Pick $288, BofA $320/$350, Barclays OW $275, Cantor all stand). **Two items dominate and they point in opposite directions, which is the honest state of the name.** **(1) THE BACKSTOP GOES TO $250BN — reported, not announced. WSJ says Nvidia is in talks to provide a $250bn backstop for [[OPENAI]] to help it lease a 10GW Ohio data-center project from SoftBank, with the project costing ~$500bn including the chips.** This is the **exact mechanism already documented on this page from SemiAnalysis's "AI Project Trinity" (07-06), scaled to one counterparty and one site, at roughly an order of magnitude above anything previously disclosed** — NVDA guarantees the lease, SoftBank owns the asset, OpenAI takes the capacity, NVDA books the chips. **Status is talks per WSJ; not confirmed by NVDA, OpenAI or SoftBank, and NOT carried here as a commitment.** JPM's framing is the one worth keeping, because it inverts the usual read: this is a *test* of the tape, not a catalyst — "the sum of money in discussion is gargantuan… **provides yet another test of whether 'good news' around capex can lift semi stocks. If another $250bn guarantee isn't enough, then well… you really have to wonder what is**" — with the SOX already **-4.3% Friday and -19% off the mid-June high**. It will, in JPM's words, "invariably raise circular financing debates again." **(2) THE MEMORY BILL IS NOW VISIBLY CUTTING NVDA'S OWN SPECS, and this is the clearest margin-transfer mechanism yet logged on this page.** Jefferies' O'Mara, citing WCCFTECH: after the reported **RTX 50 ramp delay on memory costs** and the **SOCAMM spec cut**, NVDA is **now also cutting the memory spec on NVL72** — because **"memory is the majority of the BOM on VR200."** The dispersion in sell-side inputs is the striking part: rack-cost models are running on **HBM $/GB assumptions from ~$20 (Jefferies' own Sherman, which O'Mara says is too low) to $53/GB in 2027 — a ~2.7x spread** — with one broker at **~$9.1m per rack**. O'Mara's conclusion: "Nvidia has a memory problem in its BOM and earnings structure… trying to address this by reducing the specs across multiple products." Trade-press + desk opinion, not NVDA disclosure — but it is the first *observable* evidence that the memory upcycle this book is long elsewhere ([[MU]], [[SKHYNIX]], [[SNDK]]) is being paid for partly out of NVDA's bill of materials. **(3) A clean policy split now sits between the compute layer and the model layer: NVDA published an open letter urging Washington NOT to impose sweeping restrictions on open-weight models (with MSFT co-leading, post-Kimi K3), while [[OPENAI]] and [[ANTHROPIC]] are reported to be lobbying FOR restrictions** ("increasingly rattled by China's advances"; the administration leaning toward per-model national-security review rather than a blanket ban — NYT). Consistent with UBS's read that at the GPU layer open-model traction reallocates rather than reduces demand and is **"bullish Nvidia."** **(4) Two expert calls with hard engineering numbers: next-gen GPUs reaching up to 2,400W TDP with D2C liquid cooling the current standard and advanced alternatives (two-phase immersion, TEC, new TIMs) "two to three years away" from commercial deployment; and the cost governor that cooling "should not exceed 9-10% of total data center operating or CapEx costs."** Separately, an MSFT data-center program manager puts **solid-state transformers as the eventual 800V DC standard but "not… widespread adoption before 2030-2032"** — ⚠️ **materially later than the vendor timeline on [[GEV]]'s page** (5MW indoor SST shipping to a hyperscaler this year, 6MW outdoor over 18 months, revenue "'27 and beyond"). Logged as a vendor-vs-buyer gap, not resolved. **(5) New non-tech verticals: BMY deploying Vera Rubin racks and a 5-year LLY/NVDA agreement** — pharma is new to this page — plus real-world token evidence from **AT&T at ~1tn+ tokens/month / ~16.4tn annualized, >100 GenAI models in production (~40% on AMD hardware)**. Also: **Feynman GPU + Rosa CPU targeting TSMC A16 around 2028** (Commercial Times, trade press). BofA's **NVDA buy-side bogey poll is open into the 8/26 AMC print**. 2026-07-26⭐ MU(/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (BofA Buy $1,550, TD Cowen Buy $1,600, Mizuho, Susquehanna, MS all stand). **The competitive side of this page changed materially this weekend, and it did so through a bull note on the competitor rather than a bear note on MU. The 06-29 KIS rebuttal still stands as the counter (China restricts foreign supply anyway; CXMT shifted LPDDR→DDR for domestic DCs) — so this changes no supply yet, but it does establish that customer pressure has reached the White House, in the same week as the CXMT initiation and IPO. **Third, the bullish half of Nomura's own work is the strongest demand statement now on this page and it comes from the competitor's analyst: global memory usage rising more than SEVENFOLD over 2026-30F (60%+ bit CAGR) "even after accounting for potential adoption of memory-efficiency technologies (discounting memory usage by 4x)," against all memory makers expanding at only a 30-40% bit CAGR — "that could still be insufficient."** A 4x efficiency haircut still leaves demand outrunning supply by a wide margin; that is the tightest formulation of the structural bull case on file. **Fourth, restated with the consensus gap explicit: BofA/Simon Woo's July checks have Tier-2 OEMs and module makers paying 15-20% higher PC DRAM contract prices m/m, implying +30-40% q/q against TrendForce's +13-18% for 3Q ex-HBM** — and the tape is paying for it, with **MU +8% last week (STX +8%, WDC +9%, SNDK +6%) while the SOX sat -19% off its high.** Also net-new from Arya's defence: **Kimi K3 requires ~1.4TB of HBM per serving instance despite ultra-low API pricing**, and open-weight models "multiply memory demand because every customer deployment creates a new memory endpoint" — the Jevons argument stated as architecture rather than elasticity. ⚠️ One data-integrity note: Nomura's quoted **closing price of CNY8.66 against a CNY116 TP** cannot be reconciled with a 20x-2028F-EPS-of-CNY5.8 derivation; the stock listed 7/27, so the close is pre-listing/stale and only the TP is usable. 2026-07-26⭐ INTC**/wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch). 4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (UBS Neutral $121, MS EW $84, JPM UW $85, Barclays EW $100, BofA Buy $160, Arete Neutral $99 all stand). **The material item is UBS's first full-length EMIB-T-vs-CoPoS deep dive, and its contribution is to make the yield picture on this page WORSE-defined, not better — which is the honest outcome and is recorded as such.** UBS says Intel "must lift **EMIB-T packaging from ~90-92% validation yields** toward high-volume manufacturing levels," and that **EMIB-T substrate fabrication yields "may currently be at ~50%, vs >80% for HPC ABF substrates."** Set that against the marks already on the page — **Fubon 60-70% (06-05), KeyBanc "reached 98%" (07-24), ex-Intel CVP Kaizad "mature yield 98-99%" (07-16)** — and the **packaging-yield estimates now span 60% to 99%, while substrate estimates span ~50% (UBS) to 60-70% (KeyBanc)**. Two things make this tractable rather than noise: UBS's figure is the **only one explicitly labelled *validation* yield** (which may reconcile part of the spread against production-yield marks), and UBS's own base case **routes around the substrate problem rather than solving it — "capacity expansion at Ibiden could offset lower yields and support selected products like Google's TPU v9."** So the constraint identified on 07-25 (substrates, not bridges) survives, with a lower number attached and a named workaround. **Not resolved. The 95%+ Google TPU-V9 gate remains the single item on which the Foundry thesis turns, and this page now carries three incompatible readings of it.** Net-new sizing: **advanced-packaging TAM US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR**, with UBS's *bullish* Intel stress test at **13% industry share / US$16bn of sales by 2030 (~24mn accelerator dies)** — a case in which [[TSM]] still compounds advanced-packaging sales at 50% and holds 65-70% share. **EMIB-T's structural advantages, per UBS: interposer elimination (structural cost), and scalability to ultra-large packages by building directly on large substrates — with MP targeted H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028.** And the two Intel-specific risks UBS names that were not on this page: **"integration between the foundry (TSMC) and back-end (Intel) processes, as well as yield responsibility"** — a contracting problem (who eats a yield loss when TSMC owns the die and Intel owns the package), not a technical one, and arguably the harder of the two to fix. Rating stays Neutral **explicitly on valuation**, not on execution. **Second net-new thread — glass core substrates now have a dated Intel ramp: Lens Tech (300433.SZ) is partnering with Intel on glass core with TGVs, pilot line ready by end-2026 and "more meaningful volume shipments might start from 2H27"** (MS/Derrick Yang), possibly with Korean players too. That is the structural answer to the substrate-yield constraint (lower signal loss, CTE matching against warpage, mechanical strength at panel scale), and on MS's checks Intel is **~12-18 months ahead of the TSMC/Ibiden glass path (Innolux MP 2H28-2029; BOE capacity 2027 for 2028 MP)** — the same ordering UBS gives for EMIB-T vs CoPoS. MS's caveat kept: helps sentiment, "more meaningful revenue contribution should take time," hurdles are "process control instead of pure equipment capability." **Third: 14A described as ahead of where 18A was — "defect density and transistor performance are outpacing 18A development"** (Jefferies), with Intel "aggressively locking in tool purchase orders and accelerating cleanroom buildouts," 14A "likely to be a material user of high-NA," and [[ASML]] revenue from Intel "subdued this year but likely to accelerate in 2027." The gate is unchanged and worth repeating: **the WFE step-up requires a named 14A customer, and official C27 guidance is ~6 months out.** **Fourth, the tape, recorded because it is the actual outcome: INTC closed -8% Friday having been +5% pre-market on results JPM's Harlan Sur said "cleared expectations by wide margins" — the SOX is -19% from its mid-June high, CPUs have derated -21% since June 22, and INTC is -34% since the Tech-Momentum ATH.** JPM's positioning work explains part of it — aggregate equity positioning light (US TPM 19th %ile) but **global semis crowded at the 98th %ile on both net exposure and L/S ratios**. Cross-filed [[TSM]], [[ASML]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[GOOG]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md).

2026-07-26⭐ GLW(/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 1 intra-quarter row. Nothing superseded.** **A new glass-intensive semiconductor end market is forming and the named participants are Asian display-chain incumbents, not Corning — logged as an open competitive question rather than an assumed win.** MS (Derrick Yang): **Lens Tech announced a partnership with Intel on glass core substrates with TGVs — pilot line ready by end-2026 and "more meaningful volume shipments might start from 2H27,"** possibly with Korean partners too; **Innolux + TSMC + Ibiden** at MP 2H28 or 2029; **BOE** with capacity in 2027 for 2028 MP, "aspiration to finish the whole glass core ABF substrate process"; **AUO** on glass for LEO antenna modules and micro-LED optical. The design-in rationale is squarely a materials argument — glass offers **"cost advantages from larger dimensions… better electrical performance, leading to lower signal loss, less mismatch of coefficients of thermal expansion… to mitigate the warpage issue, and greater mechanical strength"** — and **the value capture hinges on process control, not equipment: "the requirement for precision and density is much higher than what they used to deal with… if they can continue to improve the yield, we believe they will likely take a relatively big portion of the value added in the mass production stage."** The reason to track it here: **glass core is the structural answer to the ~50% ABF-substrate-yield constraint on Intel's EMIB-T and to CoPoS warpage at TSMC** (see [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md)), so the TAM is real and growing with advanced packaging — the question is whether Corning is in it. GLW reports **7/28 BMO.** Cross-filed [[INTC]], [[TSM]].
2026-07-26⭐ GEV/wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch). **4 intra-quarter rows. Nothing superseded** — but four call details are net-new and two of them directly address the fear the stock sold off on. **(1) Equipment pricing is now quantified: "1H26 orders were priced more than 20% above 4Q25 orders," with 2H gas equipment orders expected at a $/kW "at the higher end of the range of 10 to 20 points versus 4Q'25."** First hard price-realisation number on this page, against a dated base — the margin guide is being driven by price, not just volume. **(2) The 30 GW capacity target finally has its baseline, and it changes the read: it is a raise from a 24 GW FY28 plan, "through LEAN and add'l machinery."** The stock fell **-9%** on the print partly because a new ~30 GW capacity target revived overcapacity fears; a **+25% productivity raise off an existing 24 GW plan is a very different thing from a greenfield capital commitment**, and the page previously carried only the 30 GW endpoint. **(3) The forward book runs further than the page had it: "over half of '31 GWs will be on contract by the end of the year with active discussions for 2032 and beyond,"** and on peak-orders — **"continue to see very healthy growth above 125 gigawatts in '27 relative to year-end '26… confident in continuing to grow the contracted backlog for the better part of the next six quarters… even with the higher output."** **(4) Segment guidance for 2026: Power revenue +18-20% at 17-19% EBITDA margins; Electrification $14.5-15B revenue at 18-20% EBITDA margins; Wind revenue down LDD with ~$400mm of EBITDA losses.** Also logged: **~100 customers in 26 countries, 80% traditional / 20% data centers**, agreements into 2031, international pipeline (Taiwan, Saudi, Mexico, SE Asia, Qatar), and aeroderivatives explicitly framed as a **bridge** to heavy-duty rather than a substitute. **⚠️ One genuine cross-source gap logged rather than resolved — the SST adoption curve.** Management now describes **two distinct hyperscaler programs (a 5MW indoor unit shipping later this year for testing, and a 6MW outdoor unit with a different hyperscaler over ~18 months), both R&D-shared, with revenue "'27 and beyond."** But a **Microsoft data-centre program manager (AlphaSense expert call, 07-26, filed on [[NVDA]]) believes SST "will eventually become the industry standard for 800V DC" but "does not expect widespread adoption before 2030-2032,"** with 800V still "limited to pilot deployments" and most data centres on AC. GEV is selling into 2027; the buyer is planning for 2030-2032. Both can be true (pilots '27, standard '30-'32) but the implied revenue slopes differ a lot. Filed to [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). **Finally, a larger US supply-gap frame from BofA's oil-and-gas team: "US electricity demand requires the equivalent of 177GW of new capacity between 2026-30, but utilities are likely to supply just 93 gigawatts, leaving a gap exceeding 80GW,"** with **"hyperscalers plan[ning] to spend over $5trn in the next five years, yet only ~9% of US data centers have onsite generation"** and an 84GW opportunity if all behind-the-meter demand were met by gas. Note the source's own interest — the note is a Buy case for CVX/WMB/KGS/PUMP/LBRT as onsite-generation suppliers, i.e. partly substitution *away* from grid-scale CCGT — but the 177-vs-93GW gap is larger than the ">100GW shortfall" this page already carries from Obin's 07-08 "Power gap." 2026-07-25⭐ TSM**/wiki-ingest (Outlook). 1 intra-quarter row — Chipstrat · Austin Lyons, "TSMC could spend $10B more on N2 capacity. Why won't they?" (2026-07-24). Additive; no rating, PT, estimate or capacity mark superseded** (independent research letter, no rating). Folded because it supplies the quantified version of the under-investment question the page has only gestured at: TSMC spent **53% of revenue on capex in the pandemic supercycle vs 34-36% now**, on ~$100bn/yr of operating cash flow — "matching the 2021 playbook today would mean **another $30 billion a year**," and since fabs are 10-year+ assets, "**today's CapEx is TSMC's opinion on demand for the next decade**." That is a genuine counterweight to the unanimously bullish 2027-pricing flow already on the page (Fubon/Redburn/UBS/JPM). Other net-new items: every TSMC node has debuted at **~$1bn of revenue**, so flat debut dollars against rising wafer prices implies **shrinking debut volume** — N2's debut is "the fewest, most expensive wafers TSMC has ever sold"; **N5 remains the largest node family at 33%** six years on because it is the node the current AI fleet was designed into (Blackwell N4P, TPUs, Trainium, most custom XPUs); the **Q3 N2 GM dilution guide worsened to 3-4pts from the 2-3pts guided in April on a steeper ramp**; gross profit **~$27.2bn in Q2 → ~$29.8bn implied Q3 → ~$120bn annualised**; **67.7% may be the GM high-water mark until ~2028**, with the falsifiable test that **a Q3 print above 66.5% means N2 pricing is covering its own ramp** — logged as the cleanest forward read on the pricing debate. Net-new management quote from the 2Q26 call: **C.C. Wei — A14 will be "an even larger and long-lasting node for TSMC than N2, just like a 2-nanometer technology is a larger and longer-lasting node than 3-nanometer."** Also: **three greenfield N3 fabs approved** (Taiwan, Arizona, Japan) as Rubin-class parts move to N3 through 2027; N3 GM to **cross the corporate average in 2H26** with its 2022 tools depreciating off in 2027. Source is **paywalled past the margin section** — the capex-gap quantification and pricing sections were not readable and are not represented here.

2026-07-25⭐ INTC**/wiki-ingest (Outlook). Four additions off the Q2-print aftermath; no rating, PT or estimate superseded (all houses' marks unchanged from the 07-24 sweep).** The material change is to the **EMIB-T yield debate**, which the page has carried as the central gate on the Foundry-packaging thesis. **(1) KeyBanc · John Vinh (via Semiecosystem/LaPedus, 2026-07-24) — new full-log row (07-24, supply/bull) + the Advanced packaging Signal row re-marked "⚠ nuance" → "⚠→✓ tilting."** Vinh says **EMIB-T yields "have reached 98%"** — which would clear the **95%+ bar Google set for TPU V9 production** and contradicts **Fubon's 60-70% EMIB read (06-05)** and Fubon's 07-08 judgement that 95%+ by mid-2027 was "very challenging." Both prior views are retained on the page unchanged; the new figure is logged as a contradiction to resolve, not a replacement, because it is second-hand through a trade newsletter and does not specify line vs packaged-unit yield. **The bottleneck moves upstream: "INTC is supply constrained on substrates as yields are at 60-70%"** — same substrate/T-glass gate management named for 2H capacity. Also from Vinh, net-new: **18A yields 85% from 65% q/q** benchmarked for the first time against **TSMC N2 at 90% and Samsung SF2 at 50-60%** (the page had KeyBanc's ">85%" from 07-14 but no peer benchmark); **14A "progressing better than 18A" on defect densities, MP 2H28**; the broadest named foundry design-win list yet — **AAPL on both 18A and 14A for low-end M-series MacBook/iPad processors**, plus AMD, NVDA, MRVL, MSFT, MU, OpenAI (Vinh himself flags it is "unclear if Intel is providing foundry and/or packaging services"); **EMIB-T packaging wins from Amazon, Google and Microsoft**; **AI PC processor sales +26% in the quarter**; and **foundry equipment orders upwardly revised by 30-40%** with 18A and Intel 3 (Granite Rapids) capacity to expand significantly — a WFE read-through cross-referenced to KLAC/LRCX/ASML. The AAPL claim was also filed to [AAPL.md](AAPL.md). **(2) Semiecosystem/LaPedus row expanded** with the GAAP and Foundry-cumulative figures the 07-24 pass omitted: **GAAP net loss $10.848B** in Q2 (vs $3.024B a year ago); Foundry **op loss $2.1B on $5.8B** (vs $2.4B on $5.4B in Q1), the **10th consecutive Foundry loss**, **~$28B cumulative since 1Q24**; external third-party foundry + A/T revenue **$293M in Q2 / $467M in 1H26 vs $22M / $53M year-ago**. **(3) Barclays · Tom O'Malley sales email (07-24)** — new full-log row (produto/mixed) carrying one question absent from the published EW/$100 note: "curious why INTC hasn't announced an **internal compute tile for 18A for server** — would imagine they need to do that before you can have more confidence in 14A." 2026-07-25⭐ GOOG**3 intra-quarter rows + the "Frozen" catalyst bullet specified. Additive; no rating, PT or estimate superseded.** **(1) "Frozen" → "Frozen v2", now specified** (Jefferies · Blayne Curtis, 2026-07-24, citing The Information 2026-07-20). The page carried only "a new Google-designed chip to run its AI models much more efficiently." Net-new: the chip **hardwires elements of Gemini into silicon**, cutting calculations and data movement per query; Google engineers project **6-10x more tokens per unit of power vs the latest TPUs**; **C28 deployment**; possibly a **MRVL design (unconfirmed)**. Three constraints logged with it, because they are what stop this being modelled as a TPU event: Frozen is "**more experimental rather than a replacement for the TPU program**," **volumes well below TPU levels**, and **flexibility limited to Gemini versions sharing the same core architecture** — a model-specific ASIC that obsoletes on the next architecture change. Framed on the page as a **cost-per-token option, not a roadmap event**. Cross-filed to [MRVL.md](MRVL.md). **(2) UBS · Christina Dwyer, TMI Midday (07-24)** — the best read on post-print positioning: a "**buyers strike**" with no major sellers, and, critically, **2027 EPS has not cratered — buy-side models cluster $15.50-16.00 at ~20x, i.e. roughly where the stock trades** — while **2028 EPS is "all over the map (some modeling as low as $14)" on the sustainability of Anthropic spend**. Recorded because it reframes the bear case: the market is not disputing 2027 economics, it is disputing **customer concentration in 2028** — a materially different thing from the capex/FCF framing the tape used. Counter-signal from the same desk: **+$53m of combined retail net buying in GOOGL/GOOG on 07-23, the largest since Feb 2020 and 4th largest on record** (data from Aug 2013). Also logged from her VZ call notes: a **$1B dark fiber deal with Google**, with revenue recognised "over the course of the next few decades." **(3) BofA · Brian Fenske / Villani, "Learnings from Earnings Part II" (07-24)** — the desk's explicit "**Defend**" call, with the four ROI counters (**existing Cloud customers spent >50% more than committed; $257bn backlog converting over 8 quarters; Cloud margins +270bps q/q; external TPU sales ramping into year-end**) against the bear set, plus the catalyst path (Gemini 3.5 Pro / Gemini 4 in the fall, new AI ad formats, Universal Shopping Cart). The number flagged as the most uncomfortable in the print and worth tracking: **"Bridge Capacity" referencing a June SpaceX deal at as much as $50bn/GW vs Google's own capacity at $16bn/GW** — a ~3x spread whose one-off-vs-run-rate status drives the whole go-forward margin question.
2026-07-25⭐ GEV/wiki-ingest (Outlook) — **2 log rows; additive, no rating, PT or estimate superseded.** Both are BofA read-throughs relayed in Nicholas Villani's "US Insight Daily" (2026-07-24), and both bear directly on the **"peak order demand"** bear point that drove the -9% post-print reaction. **(1) BofA · Alex Jones, Siemens Energy note** — the first quantified industry capacity-vs-demand balance on this page. Net-new: **GE Vernova plans gas-turbine capacity of 30GW by 2030**, against BofA's forecast of **industry demand of 115-120GW annually 2026-28 vs expected capacity of ~102GW**, with **servicing the growing installed base consuming up to 25% of peak OEM capacity by 2035**. Jones calls the oversupply concern "**misplaced**" and sees continued pricing power and upside to consensus (Siemens Energy PO **€260, ~70% upside** — his highest-conviction idea). **The implication worth carrying: if the binding constraint is capacity rather than demand, the 125GW-by-2026 order guide reads as supply rationing, not demand deceleration** — which is a direct answer to the "is Orders even the right KPI?" question the desk raised on the print. Logged as a competitor-note read-through, not a GEV call. **(2) BofA cross-sector power team + Ross Fowler's NARUC 2026 takeaways** — following the prior week's **100GW gap between power demand and supply through 2030**, the deeper cut on behind-the-meter (the "only option," since in-front-of-the-meter is spoken for and the grid is maxxed out) finds **158GW of announced BTM projects but only 30-50% expected to get through — a residual >50GW gap**, with **Chevron and Williams the likely main deliverers**. From NARUC, Fowler reports regulators shifting from "demand only" to "**show me they're real**," with **speed-to-market now the critical bottleneck** and execution mattering more than opportunity. Both support the same conclusion: **deliverability, not appetite, is the constraint** — good for pricing and backlog quality, cautionary on the conversion rate of announced GW into orders.
